### 2.6.    Gas and ion chromatography

For gas chromatography analysis, water samples were thawed at room temperature then transferred into [SOURCE: Page_117]

internal flask gases and 50 mL of Helium was added. The flasks were incubated at 60°C for 3h at [SOURCE: Page_117]

150rpm. After incubation, gas present in water samples were measured using a gas chromatograph (µGC [SOURCE: Page_117]

R990, SRA Instrument, Marcy L’Etoile, France) with MS5A SS columns TCD detector [SOURCE: Page_117]

(10m*0.25mm*30µm BF) and PPQ (µm 10m*0.25mm*8µm) and 48 channels custom automatic sample [SOURCE: Page_117]

changer (SRA Instrument, Marcy L’Etoile, France). A calibration mix solution was prepared using [SOURCE: Page_117]

helium combined with each dissolved gas, with concentrations ranging from 0 to 14 000 ppm for CO 2, [SOURCE: Page_117]

0 to 50 000 ppm for N2O, 0 to 100 000 ppm for CH$_4$, 0 to 79 % for N$_2$ and 0 to 21% for O$_2$. The ions [SOURCE: Page_117]

present in water samples were analyzed by an ionic chromatograph (AQUION) with automatic sample [SOURCE: Page_117]

115 [SOURCE: Page_118]

changer (AS-AP 120). Anion concentrations were measured on pre-column and column (AS$_9$-HC, [SOURCE: Page_118]

4*250 mm) at 30°C and eluted with 9 mM Na^2CO$_3$ with a flow rate of 1 mL/min and a 4 mm carbonate [SOURCE: Page_118]

suppressor (AERS 500). Cation concentrations were evaluated on pre-column and column (CS-19, [SOURCE: Page_118]

4*250 mm) at 40°C and eluted with 30 mM methanesulfonic acid with a flow rate of 1 mL/min and a 4 [SOURCE: Page_118]

mm suppressor (CDRS 600). Both ions were detected with a conductimeter (Thermofischer Electron [SOURCE: Page_118]

SAS Courtaboeuf 91941). Calibration standards were prepared for each ion with concentrations ranging [SOURCE: Page_118]

from 0 (ultra-pure water only) to 500 mg/L. Anions (F-, Cl-, NO$_2$-, Br-, NO$_3$-, PO43-, SO43-) were prepared [SOURCE: Page_118]

by diluting a more concentrated stock solution (1 g/L) from NaF, KCl, NaNO$_2$, KBr, KNO$_3$, [SOURCE: Page_118]

Na^3PO$_4$,12H2O and K2SO$_4$. Cations (Na+, NH$_4$+, K+, Mg$_2$+, Ca$_2$+) were prepared by diluting a more [SOURCE: Page_118]

concentrated stock solution (1 g/L) from NaCl, (NH$_4$)2SO$_4$, KCl, MgCl$_2$,6H2O and CaCl$_2$,2H2O. [SOURCE: Page_118]

### 2.7.    UPLC-HRMS analysis

In these analyses, the 23 gardens were classified into three groups according to their proximity to [SOURCE: Page_118]

different pollution sources and the presence of atmospheric pollutants (Table 1). An aliquot of each [SOURCE: Page_118]

water sample (5 mL) was prepared by liquid-liquid extraction with 5 mL acetonitrile acidified with 0.1 [SOURCE: Page_118]

% formic acid. Each sample was fortified with a labelled internal standard (diuron-d^6), then [SOURCE: Page_118]

ultrasonicated, centrifuged and frozen overnight at -18°C. A 2.5 mL aliquot was retrieved. A volume of [SOURCE: Page_118]

500 µL of each sample extract was pooled to prepare a quality control sample (QC). Then the 2 mL left [SOURCE: Page_118]

extracts were dried under a slight nitrogen flow at 40°C. Each dry extract was reconstituted in 200 µL [SOURCE: Page_118]

of water/methanol 90/10, v/v. Moreover, to ensure the reliability of the results, each sample was [SOURCE: Page_118]

extracted and analyzed 3 times. Extracts with ultra-pure water were prepared with the same protocol and [SOURCE: Page_118]

considered as blank matrix. Analyses by LC-HRMS were performed on an Ultimate 3000 UHPLC [SOURCE: Page_118]

system (Thermo Scientific®, MA, USA) coupled to a quadrupole time-of-flight mass spectrometer [SOURCE: Page_118]

(QToF) (Maxis Plus, Bruker Daltonics®, Bremen, Germany) equipped with an electrospray ionization [SOURCE: Page_118]

interface (ESI). Analyses were carried out in reverse phase (elution gradient) employing an Acclaim [SOURCE: Page_118]

116 [SOURCE: Page_119]

RSLC 120 C18 column (2.2 μm, 100 × 2.1 mm, ThermoScientific®), protected with a KrudKatcher [SOURCE: Page_119]

Ultra In-Line Filter guard column from Phenomenex (Torrance, CA, USA) and heated at 30°C. The [SOURCE: Page_119]

injected volume was 5 μL. Mobile phases consisted of: an aqueous phase (90%/10% ultrapure [SOURCE: Page_119]

water/methanol mixture with 5 mM ammonium formate and 0.01% formic acid) and an organic phase [SOURCE: Page_119]

(methanol with 5 mM ammonium formate and 0.01% formic acid). All extracts were analyzed in [SOURCE: Page_119]

positive electrospray ionization with the following settings: capillary voltage of 3600 V, end plate offset [SOURCE: Page_119]

of 500 V, nebulizer pressure of 3 bar (N$_2$), drying gas of 9 L/min (N$_2$), and drying temperature of 200 [SOURCE: Page_119]

°C. An external calibration of exact masses was systematically performed at the beginning of each run, [SOURCE: Page_119]

using a solution of sodium formate and acetate (0.5 mL of 1 M NaOH, 25 μL of formic acid and 75 μL [SOURCE: Page_119]

of acetic acid in in H2O/isopropanol 50/50 v/v), generating cluster ions [M+H] + in the range 90.9766– [SOURCE: Page_119]

948.8727 Da with high precision calibration (HPC) mode at a search range ± 0.05 m/z. Accepted [SOURCE: Page_119]

standard deviations were inferior to 0.5 ppm. QCs were run several times at the beginning of the [SOURCE: Page_119]

analytical sequence to equilibrate the column and at regular intervals throughout the sequence to check [SOURCE: Page_119]

for instrument drift and to control analytical repeatability and sensitivity. Instrument control, data [SOURCE: Page_119]

acquisition, and processing were performed using OTOF Control and HystarTM (v^4.1, Bruker [SOURCE: Page_119]

Daltonics®) software. Samples were randomly analyzed in full-MS scan over a m/z range of 50-1000 [SOURCE: Page_119]

Da. QCs were also analyzed in DDA (Data Dependent Analysis), a mode with alternative acquisition in [SOURCE: Page_119]

MS and MSMS, with predefined parameters. The data preprocessing was performed with W4M and [SOURCE: Page_119]

Metaboscape (Giacomoni et al. 2015). A feature matrix was generated for each sequence. It contained [SOURCE: Page_119]

1382 features for which the mass to charge ratio m/z in ion adduct form, the retention time RT of each [SOURCE: Page_119]

detected peak, the intensity in each sample. Each feature was then normalized to the intensity of diuron- [SOURCE: Page_119]

d^6. It was discarded from the dataset when the coefficient of variation in the QCs (CVQC) was over [SOURCE: Page_119]

30%. Missing intensities in a replicate were replaced by the mean intensities in the two others replicate [SOURCE: Page_119]

or by 0 if not available. Then for each group of gardens (Table 1), a detection rate was calculated. If it [SOURCE: Page_119]

was less than 100%, the feature wasn’t considered afterwards. Signals that were not significantly higher [SOURCE: Page_119]

in samples compared to blanks (intensity ratio > 10), and were not significantly different between C and [SOURCE: Page_119]

NC (t-test p>0.05) were not considered. Annotation and identification were performed using [SOURCE: Page_119]

Metaboscape and MetFrag (Wolf et al. 2010) an in silico fragmentation tool, by comparing experimental [SOURCE: Page_119]

117 [SOURCE: Page_120]

MSMS spectra. Using these filters, a list of 147 LC-HRMS signals was kept on the 1382 features. Finally [SOURCE: Page_120]

markers were putatively identified according to the level of identification confidence from 1 to 5 [SOURCE: Page_120]

(Schymanski et al. 2015) (Table 2). [SOURCE: Page_120]

### 2.8.    Total organic carbon analysis and measurement

Total Organic Carbon (TOC) was performed on a Shimadzu TOC 5050 (Kyoto, Japan). From 16 to 54 [SOURCE: Page_120]

µL of samples were injected in a vertical combustion tube hold at 680°C and half filled with platinum- [SOURCE: Page_120]

based catalysis in a flow of 150 mL/min. Total Carbon (TC) was converted into CO$_2$ and measured on [SOURCE: Page_120]

Non-Dispersive Infrared (NDIR) CO$_2$ detector. Inorganic Carbon (IC) was measured by the same [SOURCE: Page_120]

amount of injection in a vessel containing phosphoric acid at a concentration of 25%. Carbon contained [SOURCE: Page_120]

in carbonates was converted into CO$_2$ and quantified on the same infrared detector. TOC concentration [SOURCE: Page_120]

was obtained by the difference between TC and IC concentrations. [SOURCE: Page_120]

### 2.9.    Statistical and data analysis

Data analysis and statistical tests were carried out with R Statistical Software (v^4.1.1, R Core Team [SOURCE: Page_120]

2018), using the vegan package for diversity analyses (Oksanen et al. 2018) and the ggplot^2 package for [SOURCE: Page_120]

graphical representations (Wickham 2016). Heatmap graphs were plotted with the complexHeatmap [SOURCE: Page_120]

package (Gu et al. 2016). Prokaryotic, fungal and eukaryotic communities within-sample diversity also [SOURCE: Page_120]

referred as alpha-diversity was estimated with the Shannon index (H’) while community dissimilarity [SOURCE: Page_120]

also referred as beta diversity was estimated with the Bray-Curtis index. Shannon index was tested using [SOURCE: Page_120]

either nonparametric tests (Kruskal-Wallis and paired Wilcoxon signed rank tests with Benjamini- [SOURCE: Page_120]

Hochberg correction) for bacteria or parametric tests (ANOVA and Tukey HSD post hoc tests) for fungi [SOURCE: Page_120]

and other microeukaryotes. Beta diversity was analyzed using permutational multivariate analysis [SOURCE: Page_120]

118 [SOURCE: Page_121]

(adonis^2, R package vegan) (Legendre and Anderson 1999). Non-metric multidimensional scaling [SOURCE: Page_121]

(NMDS) plots using Bray-Curtis dissimilarity matrices were used to visualize beta diversity of [SOURCE: Page_121]

microbial communities. Comparison of physicochemical parameters and organic molecule occurrence [SOURCE: Page_121]

between NC and C was performed with partial least squares discriminant analysis (PLS-DA) with [SOURCE: Page_121]

mixOmics, R package (Rohart et al. 2017). The influence of the breeding site colonization status on the [SOURCE: Page_121]

5 most discriminant physicochemical parameters was tested by linear mixed model (lmm) after [SOURCE: Page_121]

correcting for variations between inter-individual garden effect. Considering organic molecules, data [SOURCE: Page_121]

were not analyzed with lmm because the residuals of the model were heteroskedasts and did follow a [SOURCE: Page_121]

normal distribution. We therefore tested them using Kruskal-Wallis rank sum test, a non-parametric test. [SOURCE: Page_121]

Principal Components Analysis (PCA) were performed for NC and C samples independently in function [SOURCE: Page_121]

of either physicochemical combined with pollution variables or organic molecule water occurrence. [SOURCE: Page_121]

Monte Carlo test on the sum of the singular values of a procustean rotation was performed to test the [SOURCE: Page_121]

concordance of each dataset. To analyze whether the abiotic factors of NC and C (either [SOURCE: Page_121]

physicochemical and pollutant variables or organic molecule occurrence) were correlated with the [SOURCE: Page_121]

microbial communities, we performed the same analysis. Then, we asked what was the proportion of [SOURCE: Page_121]

the microbial communities within NC and C that could be explained by major variations in the abiotic [SOURCE: Page_121]

factors. To that end, we have used the two first components (PC1_poll/PC2_poll or PC1_mp/PC2_mp, [SOURCE: Page_121]

for physicochemical and pollution variables or organic molecules, respectively) of the abiotic factors’ [SOURCE: Page_121]

PCA as an explanatory variable for a PERMANOVA analysis of the microbial communities Bray Curtis [SOURCE: Page_121]

dissimilarities. Then, we performed distance-based Redundancy Analyses (dbRDA) as constrained [SOURCE: Page_121]

multivariate analyses to represent the microbial OTUs that were mostly influenced by [SOURCE: Page_121]

PC1_poll/PC2_poll or PC1_mp/PC2_mp. Those OTUs were correlated with PC1_poll/PC2_poll or [SOURCE: Page_121]

PC1_mp/PC2_mp using Spearman’s rank correlations. [SOURCE: Page_121]

Then, the objective was to develop a method allowing to determine relationships between [SOURCE: Page_121]

variables of different natures, including categorical, numerical, and not following normal distributions, [SOURCE: Page_121]

and then to visualize them with graph representations, in which nodes represent privileged relationships [SOURCE: Page_121]

between those variables. To that end, we used a python code and visualized with the pyvis library. For [SOURCE: Page_121]

each numerical variable (OTUs, Water T°, LC-HRMS data, etc.), we converted it into a binary value, [SOURCE: Page_121]

119 [SOURCE: Page_122]

such as exceptionally high values were identified as 1, and average or low values were identified as 0. [SOURCE: Page_122]

Given the non-normal distribution of values, we used a robust variant of the z-score to identify [SOURCE: Page_122]

exceptionally high values. More precisely, we computed for each variable the so-called modified z- [SOURCE: Page_122]

score, computed using the median absolute deviation to the median instead of the standard deviation. [SOURCE: Page_122]

Each value x_i is thus transformed using the following formula: [SOURCE: Page_122]

𝑥_𝑖 − 𝑚𝑒𝑑(𝑥) 𝑀𝐴𝐷 [SOURCE: Page_122]

Where ‘med(x)’ corresponds to the median of variable ‘x’ and ‘MAD’ the median absolute deviation. [SOURCE: Page_122]

We then defined a threshold above which a value is considered exceptional. In the second step, we used [SOURCE: Page_122]

association rule scores to identify strong associations. More precisely we used Zhang’s score to assess [SOURCE: Page_122]

how         strongly      related     a     variable     is     compared        with      another       one [SOURCE: Page_122]

(https://rasbt.github.io/mlxtend/user_guide/frequent_patterns/association_rules/). Zhang’s score is [SOURCE: Page_122]

defined between -1 and +1, with 0 meaning no particular association. Here again, we defined a threshold [SOURCE: Page_122]

to keep only values above a given threshold, i.e., having a strong enough association. Zhang’s score [SOURCE: Page_122]

does not include statistical significance, so we add a statistical test (Binomial test, p-value < 0.05) a [SOURCE: Page_122]

posteriori to assess the robustness of potential associations. An edge is added to the graph if the strength [SOURCE: Page_122]

of the association is above the threshold and statistically significant. We used this method to avoid [SOURCE: Page_122]

pitfalls with existing approaches. [SOURCE: Page_122]

### 3.1.       Noncolonized and colonized waters exhibit slight differences in physicochemical and

organic molecule composition [SOURCE: Page_122]

Following our selection criteria (see the Materials and Methods section), a total of 23 gardens [SOURCE: Page_122]

were selected for water sample collection. The characterization of each garden according to the level of [SOURCE: Page_122]

atmospheric pollution, the calculated pollution variables and the distance from the garden to each [SOURCE: Page_122]

pollution source is given in Table 1. The most common containers sampled were plastic rainwater [SOURCE: Page_122]

120 [SOURCE: Page_123]

collectors (87%), followed by plastic buckets (9%) and plastic watering cans (4%) (Table 3). Water [SOURCE: Page_123]

physicochemical properties, i.e., ionic and gas composition, TOC, temperature, pH, conductivity, redox [SOURCE: Page_123]

potential, and dissolved oxygen, are all summarized in Table 3 and Table 4. We conducted a PLS-DA [SOURCE: Page_123]

supervised analysis to identify the most discriminant physicochemical parameters between C and NC [SOURCE: Page_123]

(Figure 2A). Among the physicochemical properties, N2O and CH$_4$ were significantly higher in NC than [SOURCE: Page_123]

in C according to linear mixed model (χ2 = 4.702, p =0.03 and χ2 = 4.674, p = 0.03, respectively) (Figure [SOURCE: Page_123]

2B). In fact, the average N2O and CH$_4$ were two and nearly seven times higher in NC than in C, [SOURCE: Page_123]

respectively. In a second analysis, NC and C samples were separated (Figure 2C-D) to compare their [SOURCE: Page_123]

physicochemical composition through a Procrustean analysis. The abiotic factors (physicochemical [SOURCE: Page_123]

factors and pollution variables) had no significant effects on colonization status (NC or C) (Monte Carlo [SOURCE: Page_123]

permutation test, observation = 0.345, p = 0.135). Regarding organic molecule analysis, the application [SOURCE: Page_123]

of a nontargeted approach involving liquid chromatography coupled with quadrupole time-of-flight [SOURCE: Page_123]

mass spectrometry (LC-QToF) allowed the detection of compounds that were found to be significantly [SOURCE: Page_123]

different between C and NC from the same garden (t-test p<0.05) (Supplementary Table S$_3$). Among [SOURCE: Page_123]

them, 22 features were putatively identified (Table 2). However, PLS-DA performed with all 147 [SOURCE: Page_123]

features did not reveal significant differences between C and NC regarding organic molecule presence [SOURCE: Page_123]

(p > 0.05) (Figure 3A-B). When NC and C samples were separated (Figure 3C-D) through the [SOURCE: Page_123]

Procrustes analysis, no significant effect was found depending on the colonization status (NC or C) [SOURCE: Page_123]

(Monte Carlo permutation test, observation = 0.06, p = 0.985). [SOURCE: Page_123]

### 3.2.       Fungal composition differs between noncolonized and colonized waters while

microbial community structure is similar between larvae and water samples, [SOURCE: Page_123]

regardless of colonization status [SOURCE: Page_123]

A total of 1373 bacterial OTUs belonging to 27 phyla and 329 genera were identified from the [SOURCE: Page_123]

23 gardens (23 NC, 23 C and their associated larvae). NC and C exhibited similar bacterial compositions [SOURCE: Page_123]

at the phylum level (Supplementary Figure S1A). The predominant phylum was Proteobacteria (37% [SOURCE: Page_123]

121 [SOURCE: Page_124]

and 34%), followed by Actinobacteriota (20% and 24%) and Firmicutes (17% and 12%) for NC and C, [SOURCE: Page_124]

respectively (Supplementary Figure S1A). In larvae, the most abundant bacterial phylum was [SOURCE: Page_124]

Proteobacteria (35%), followed by Actinobacteriota (24%) and Bacteroidota (17%) (Supplementary [SOURCE: Page_124]

Figure S1A). At the genus level, 40%, 34% and 31% of the bacterial genera displayed low abundance [SOURCE: Page_124]

(<1%) in NC, C and L, respectively (Supplementary Figure S1B). Among the 42 most abundant [SOURCE: Page_124]

genera, 7 were common (e.g., Rhizobium, Legionella, and Abditibacterium), and others were specific to [SOURCE: Page_124]

NC (6), C (7) and larvae (14) (Supplementary Figure S1B). Interestingly, Actinomycetospora and [SOURCE: Page_124]

Actinoplanes were abundant in C and L but in low abundance in NC (<1%). The alpha diversities of the [SOURCE: Page_124]

bacterial communities in NC and C were comparable (Wilcoxon pairwise; p = 0.53), while that in L was [SOURCE: Page_124]

significantly lower than that in C (Wilcoxon pairwise; p = 0.0012) and NC (Wilcoxon pairwise; p = [SOURCE: Page_124]

0.0005) (Supplementary Table S$_4$). Regarding the fungal community (i.e., ITS sequencing), a total of [SOURCE: Page_124]

18 gardens were analysed out of the 23 sampled gardens (with 18 NC, 18 C and their associated larvae). [SOURCE: Page_124]

If the ITS sequencing preferentially targeted and revealed the presence of fungi (56.4%, 56.6% and [SOURCE: Page_124]

57.8%), other eukaryotic kingdoms such as Viridiplantae were also detected (15.4%, 16.4% and 15.8%) [SOURCE: Page_124]

for NC, C and L, respectively, followed by 3 minority kingdoms (Rhizaria, Protista and Ichthyosporia) [SOURCE: Page_124]

(data not shown). Regarding fungal communities, a total of 716 fungal OTUs belonging to 8 phyla and [SOURCE: Page_124]

264 genera were identified. Ascomycota was the predominant phylum (47%, 55% and 72% in NC, C [SOURCE: Page_124]

and L, respectively), followed by Basidiomycota (27%, 24% and 16%) (Supplementary Figure S2A). [SOURCE: Page_124]

The phylum Rozellomycota was the third most abundant phylum in NC and C (7% and 6%) but [SOURCE: Page_124]

represented only 0.5% of the total fungi in L (Supplementary Figure S2A). At the genus level, 40%, [SOURCE: Page_124]

34% and 31% of the fungal genera displayed a low abundance (<1%) in NC, C and L, respectively [SOURCE: Page_124]

(Supplementary Figure S2B). Only Dioszegia was common to NC and C, while Phoma was shared [SOURCE: Page_124]

among NC, C and L (Supplementary Figure S2B). Interestingly, Dacrymyces and Pyrenochaetopsis [SOURCE: Page_124]

were abundant in C and L but very low in abundance in NC (0.18% and 0.04%, respectively) [SOURCE: Page_124]

(Supplementary Figure S2C). Comparison analysis performed with the Shannon index indicated no [SOURCE: Page_124]

significant differences in the alpha diversity of fungal communities between NC and C [SOURCE: Page_124]

(Tukey HSD post hoc tests, t ratio = 0.559, df = 51, p = 0.84) or between C and L [SOURCE: Page_124]

(Tukey HSD post hoc tests, t ratio = -1.914, df = 51, p = 0.14) (Supplementary Table S4). Conversely, [SOURCE: Page_124]

122 [SOURCE: Page_125]

the alpha diversity of fungal communities was significantly higher in L than in NC (Tukey HSD post [SOURCE: Page_125]

hoc test, t ratio = 2.473, df = 51, p = 0.04) (Supplementary Table S$_4$). Regarding eukaryotic [SOURCE: Page_125]

communities, a total of 739 eukaryotic OTUs belonging to 38 phyla and 181 genera were identified [SOURCE: Page_125]

among 13 gardens (13 NC and 13 C). In contrast to bacteria and fungi, the main eukaryotic phyla differed [SOURCE: Page_125]

between NC and C. The predominant phylum was Ciliophora (22.5%), followed by Chlorophyta [SOURCE: Page_125]

(18.4%) and Ochrophyta (10.2%), in NC, while the predominant phylum was Bicosoecida (19.5%), [SOURCE: Page_125]

followed by Ochrophyta (14.6%) and Chlorophyta (9.8%), in C (Supplementary Figure S3A). At the [SOURCE: Page_125]

genus level, 10.3% and 14.9% of the eukaryotic genera displayed low abundance (<1%) in NC and C, [SOURCE: Page_125]

respectively (Supplementary Figure S3B). Among the 28 most abundant genera, three were common [SOURCE: Page_125]

(Sterkiella, Scotinosphaera and Goniomonas), and others were specific to NC (13) and C (11) [SOURCE: Page_125]

(Supplementary Figure S3B). The alpha diversities of the eukaryotic communities in NC and C were [SOURCE: Page_125]

comparable (Tukey HSD post hoc test,       p   = 0.9) (Supplementary         Table    S$_4$).   Nonmetric [SOURCE: Page_125]

multidimensional scaling based on Bray‒Curtis dissimilarity revealed no differences in microbial [SOURCE: Page_125]

composition between NC and C (adonis^2-PERMANOVA, R$_2$ = 0.02, p = 0.77 for bacteria; R$_2$ = [SOURCE: Page_125]

0.04, p = 0.7 for eukaryotes) (Figure 4A, 4B and 4C). However, significant differences in fungal [SOURCE: Page_125]

composition were found between NC and C (adonis^2-PERMANOVA, R$_2$ = 0.04, p = 0.03). Moreover, [SOURCE: Page_125]

significant differences in bacterial composition were observed between NC and L (adonis^2- [SOURCE: Page_125]

PERMANOVA, R$_2$ = 0.03, p = 0.001) as well as between C and L (adonis^2-PERMANOVA, R$_2$ = [SOURCE: Page_125]

0.03, p = 0.001). Heatmaps showed contrasting patterns with some OTUs unique (specific) to certain [SOURCE: Page_125]

categories of samples or shared (OTUs that are common among several categories of samples) (Figure [SOURCE: Page_125]

4D-F). Among fungi, OTU 3 (family Didymellaceae) was present in all samples. Conversely, the [SOURCE: Page_125]

bacterial OTUs 50, 51, and 52 (family Beijerinckiaceae), 62 (family Anaeromyxobacteraceae), 84 [SOURCE: Page_125]

(family Bacillaceae), 144 (family Parachlamydiaceae) and 202 (family Clostridiaceae) as well as the [SOURCE: Page_125]

fungal OTU 54 (not identified) were found in substantial proportions in NC and C but absent from L. [SOURCE: Page_125]

Two other bacterial OTUs (OTU 13 and OTU 14) belonging to family Pseudonocardiaceae were [SOURCE: Page_125]

enriched in L compared to C [SOURCE: Page_125]

### 3.3.       Organic molecules and physicochemical properties differentially affect microbial

communities in noncolonized and colonized waters [SOURCE: Page_126]

According to previous analyses, coordinates of the two first components of PCA (PC1_poll and [SOURCE: Page_126]

PC2_poll from Figure 2C-2D and PC1_mp and PC2_mp from Figure 3C-D) were extracted and [SOURCE: Page_126]

subsequently used to reflect the distribution of samples according to either physicochemical and [SOURCE: Page_126]

pollution variables or the occurrence of organic molecules in terms their microbial composition. We [SOURCE: Page_126]

then evaluated whether those variables shape the microbial structure of NC and C. For this purpose, [SOURCE: Page_126]

distance-based redundancy analyses (db-RDAs) were conducted to maximize the separation of water [SOURCE: Page_126]

microbial communities based on differences in either their physicochemical composition and pollution [SOURCE: Page_126]

context (PC1_poll and PC2_poll) or organic molecule structure (PC1_mp and PC2_mp) (Figure 5 and [SOURCE: Page_126]

6). In colonized water samples, the structure of bacterial communities was significantly correlated with [SOURCE: Page_126]

PC1_poll        (adonis^2-PERMANOVA, R$_2$ 0.07          =    0.03, P =      0.001),          PC2_poll   (adonis^2- [SOURCE: Page_126]

PERMANOVA, R$_2$ = 0.06, P = 0.015) and PC2_mp (adonis^2-PERMANOVA, R$_2$ = 0.44, P = 0.007), [SOURCE: Page_126]

while that of fungal communities varied only according to PC1_poll (adonis^2-PERMANOVA, R$_2$ = [SOURCE: Page_126]

0.07, P = 0.006). The most important microbial OTUs involved in these separations were bacterial OTU [SOURCE: Page_126]

13 (identified as Actinomycetospora) and fungal OTU 11 (unidentified Rozellomycota), which [SOURCE: Page_126]

correlated with high values of PC1_poll (p = 0.017, Rhô= 0.5 for OTU 13 and p = 0.003, Rhô = 0.66 for [SOURCE: Page_126]

OTU 11), as well as bacterial OTU 39 (identified as Rhizobiaceae) (Figure 5A-B) and fungal OTU 44 [SOURCE: Page_126]

(identified as Ascochyta phacae), which correlated with low and high values of PC2_mp, respectively [SOURCE: Page_126]

(p = 0.008, Rhô = 0.54 for OTU 39 and p = 0.04, Rhô = 0.48 for OTU 44) (Figure 5C-D). Regarding [SOURCE: Page_126]

noncolonized water samples, the structure of microbial communities was significantly influenced by [SOURCE: Page_126]

PC1_poll       (adonis^2-PERMANOVA, R$_2$ =           0.06, P =     0.027    for        bacteria   and   adonis^2- [SOURCE: Page_126]

PERMANOVA, R$_2$ =           0.1, P =   0.04   for   eukaryotes)   as    well     as    by    PC1_mp    (adonis^2- [SOURCE: Page_126]

PERMANOVA, R$_2$ = 0.08, P = 0.025 for fungi). The most important clusters involved in these [SOURCE: Page_126]

separations were OTU 24 (identified as Haematococcus), which correlated with PC1_poll (p = 0.017, [SOURCE: Page_126]

Rhô = 0.6) (Figure 6A), and OTU 30 (unidentified Rozellomycota) and OTU 163 (identified as [SOURCE: Page_126]

124 [SOURCE: Page_127]

Neochloris aquatica), which correlated with PC1_mp (p = 0.03, Rhô = 0.5 and p = 0.04, Rhô = 0.48 for [SOURCE: Page_127]

OTU 30 and OTU 163, respectively) (Figure 6B). [SOURCE: Page_127]

### 3.4.       Organic molecules and microbial communities are highly connected in colonized water

samples [SOURCE: Page_127]

A network analysis was performed on colonized water samples to explore associations between [SOURCE: Page_127]

(Figure 7A). The second network showed module associations between 9 bacterial OTUs (i.e., 89, 93, [SOURCE: Page_127]

### 4. Discussion

Whether biotic and abiotic factors directly or indirectly impact the ability of mosquitoes to thrive [SOURCE: Page_128]

in aquatic habitats remains poorly investigated. We addressed this question by specifically studying Ae. [SOURCE: Page_128]

albopictus, one of the most invasive mosquito species in the world. As the type of container was shown [SOURCE: Page_128]

to influence water physicochemical parameters (Hery et al. 2021b), our analysis mainly focused on [SOURCE: Page_128]

rainwater collectors. Rainwater harvesting is a widely used method to collect and store rainwater for [SOURCE: Page_128]

garden water supply and leads to common breeding sites for Aedes mosquitoes (Ritchie et al. 2002; [SOURCE: Page_128]

Duval et al. 2022). We first investigated whether the presence/absence of Ae. albopictus larvae in water [SOURCE: Page_128]

habitats could be related to specific biotic and abiotic features. With the exception of N2O and CH$_4$ [SOURCE: Page_128]

concentrations, our results showed no significant differences in water physicochemical properties and [SOURCE: Page_128]

microbial composition between colonized and noncolonized water samples. We showed that the average [SOURCE: Page_128]

water temperature and pH values of colonized larval habitats were 21.6 °C and 7.4, respectively. The [SOURCE: Page_128]

temperature ranged between 15 °C and 26 °C, while the pH values were between 5.7 and 8.8. Consistent [SOURCE: Page_128]

with the findings of other studies, the average electrical conductivity and dissolved oxygen were 0.19 [SOURCE: Page_128]

S/m and 6.45 mg/L, respectively (Rao et al. 2011; Garcia-Sánchez et al. 2017; Dalpadado et al. 2022). [SOURCE: Page_128]

These values are optimal for the development of Ae. albopictus (Delatte et al. 2009; Medeiros-Sousa et [SOURCE: Page_128]

al. 2018; Reinhold et al. 2018). When measured as a percentage of the calculated (or expected) amount [SOURCE: Page_128]

at saturation, referred to as dissolved saturation (%DO), dissolved oxygen was on average 2.1%. This [SOURCE: Page_128]

contrasted with previous measures of %DO of approximately 1-1.5% in Aedes breeding sites [SOURCE: Page_128]

126 [SOURCE: Page_129]

(Gopalakrishnan et al. 2013; Medeiros-Sousa et al. 2020). Interestingly, it was previously shown that [SOURCE: Page_129]

%DO is positively correlated with mosquito larval density and can influence the forms and toxicity of [SOURCE: Page_129]

dissolved metals as well as the concentrations of certain chemicals, such as sulphides and ammonia [SOURCE: Page_129]

(Khalid et al. 1978; Gopalakrishnan et al. 2013). Regarding redox potential, higher values were observed [SOURCE: Page_129]

in Ae. aegypti breeding sites (Garcia-Sánchez et al. 2017) and could be explained by variation in [SOURCE: Page_129]

microbial communities or ionic composition (DeLaune and Reddy 2005; Søndergaard 2009). With the [SOURCE: Page_129]

exception of oxygen, dissolved gases in larval breeding sites remain poorly studied. Our study is the [SOURCE: Page_129]

first to examine the presence and concentration of several gases in Ae. albopictus water habitats. [SOURCE: Page_129]

Interestingly, it was shown that the presence of methane in the larval habitats of Culex pipiens increased [SOURCE: Page_129]

oviposition, while that of CO$_2$ did not seem to affect mosquito colonization or fitness (Bentley and Day [SOURCE: Page_129]

1989) or Aedes hatching stimulation (Judson 1960). In addition, methane and dissolved CO$_2$ exposure [SOURCE: Page_129]

could also affect water microbial communities (Brankovits et al. 2017). Nitrate, potassium and chloride [SOURCE: Page_129]

ions were detected at higher concentrations than those found in tap water, which could be linked to the [SOURCE: Page_129]

use of nitrate fertilizers and the importance of fertilizer inputs in intensive urban agriculture [SOURCE: Page_129]

(Amerasinghe et al. 1995; Panno et al. 2006; Wakejo et al. 2022). Although previous studies showed [SOURCE: Page_129]

that mosquitoes are able to reproduce over a wide range of ion concentrations (Sehgal and Pillai 1970; [SOURCE: Page_129]

Bashar et al. 2016; Garcia-Sánchez et al. 2017), the impact of water ionic composition on mosquito [SOURCE: Page_129]

physiology remains unclear. Due to confounding factors in a complex field environment, further studies [SOURCE: Page_129]

are required to investigate the impact of ionic and gas composition alone or in combination with other [SOURCE: Page_129]

physicochemical parameters on different mosquito traits. For instance, the bioavailability of [SOURCE: Page_129]

contaminants in water may be strongly influenced by the chemical environment (in particular ionic [SOURCE: Page_129]

composition) and could in turn affect mosquito larvae by either directly modulating their physiology or [SOURCE: Page_129]

indirectly modulating the microbial communities of breeding sites (Adams et al. 2020; Hery et al. [SOURCE: Page_129]

2021b). [SOURCE: Page_129]

Consistent with previous reports, the dominant bacterial phyla in colonized water samples were [SOURCE: Page_129]

Pseudomonadota      (previously    named    Proteobacteria),    Actinobacteriota    (previously    named [SOURCE: Page_129]

Actinobacteria) and Bacteroidota (previously named Bacteroides) (Wang et al. 2018; Scolari et al. [SOURCE: Page_129]

127 [SOURCE: Page_130]

2021), while Ascomycota, Basidiomycota and Rozellomycota (previously named Cryptomycota) were [SOURCE: Page_130]

the dominant fungal phyla (Tawidian et al. 2021). The microalga Picochlorum and the protozoan [SOURCE: Page_130]

flagellate Spumela were found to be specific to colonized waters. Interestingly, polysaccharides [SOURCE: Page_130]

extracted from Picochlorum were shown to exhibit antiviral activity against human enterovirus 71 (Guo [SOURCE: Page_130]

et al. 2021). It could be interesting to evaluate whether this effect persists in larvae and adult mosquitoes [SOURCE: Page_130]

and could interfere with arbovirus transmission. Surprisingly, Egizi et al. (2014) observed the opposite [SOURCE: Page_130]

tendency, with a negative correlation between the density of Culex quinquefasciatus larvae and that of [SOURCE: Page_130]

Spumela. Overall, our results confirmed that a fraction of microorganisms present in larval breeding [SOURCE: Page_130]

water are maintained in the larval stage with higher microbial diversity in water breeding sites compared [SOURCE: Page_130]

to in larvae (Dada et al. 2014; Coon et al. 2016; Wang et al. 2018; Strand 2018; Scolari et al. 2021). We [SOURCE: Page_130]

identified bacteria such as Rhizobium and fungi such as Dacrymyces and Pyrenochaetopsis that were [SOURCE: Page_130]

shared between water and larvae. However, whether these taxa play a role in mosquito biology is still [SOURCE: Page_130]

unknown. Rhizobium was listed as part of the core microbiota in different Anopheles mosquito tissues [SOURCE: Page_130]

(Tchioffi et al. 2016; Guégan et al. 2018). Another study showed that Ae. albopictus females were not [SOURCE: Page_130]

attracted to or repelled by Rhizobium huautlense isolated from canebrake bamboo, but we cannot [SOURCE: Page_130]

exclude a role in larval development (Ponnusamy et al. 2015). Interestingly, noncolonized water [SOURCE: Page_130]

exhibited higher fungal diversity and specific fungal composition as well as higher concentrations of [SOURCE: Page_130]

N2O compared to colonized samples. This could be explained by the fact that N2O production is a [SOURCE: Page_130]

widespread trait in fungi (Maeda et al. 2015). [SOURCE: Page_130]

Physicochemical parameters and microbial communities interact with each other in aquatic [SOURCE: Page_130]

ecosystems (Bojarczuk et al. 2018; Rahman et al. 2021) and can also be influenced by urban pollution. [SOURCE: Page_130]

Physicochemical factors and pollution exposure could directly alter bacterial communities by impairing [SOURCE: Page_130]

microbial physiological activities or indirectly by establishing conditions that adversely affect [SOURCE: Page_130]

microorganisms. In community gardens, rainwater tanks are mainly filled with rainwater and in some [SOURCE: Page_130]

cases may be supplemented with tap water, which can be occasionally contaminated by organic [SOURCE: Page_130]

molecules in trace quantities (Kim and Zoh 2016). Although rainwater is considered a contamination- [SOURCE: Page_130]

free source, human activities, particularly in the industrial and agricultural sectors, pollute this pure form [SOURCE: Page_130]

128 [SOURCE: Page_131]

of water (Guidotti et al. 2000; Samuel et al. 2012). Over the last decade, a wide range of contaminants [SOURCE: Page_131]

have been detected in surface water, including pharmaceutical, industrial and pesticide compounds [SOURCE: Page_131]

(Rosen 2007; Verlicchi et al. 2010; Yang et al. 2022). Some of them, such as microplastic particles and [SOURCE: Page_131]

antibiotic residues, were shown to alter water microbial communities (Qing et al. 2020; Yang et al. 2022; [SOURCE: Page_131]

Edwards et al. 2023). To the best of our knowledge, no study has characterized the presence of organic [SOURCE: Page_131]

molecules in breeding sites without a priori information. In our study, we identified organic molecules, [SOURCE: Page_131]

although uncertainties remain regarding some candidate compounds. Further studies could be conducted [SOURCE: Page_131]

using a suspect screening approach to better identify organic molecules (Pinasseau et al. 2023). Among [SOURCE: Page_131]

the identified organic molecules, we found pharmaceutical residues such as anti-inflammatories, [SOURCE: Page_131]

hormones or antioxidants (3-tert-butyl-4-hydroxyanisole), as well as fertilizers (dimethylenetriurea) and [SOURCE: Page_131]

plastics (phthalate derived). Due to their high prevalence in aquatic ecosystems, further experiments are [SOURCE: Page_131]

needed to test the influence of these compounds on the physiology of Ae. albopictus (Yang et al. 2022). [SOURCE: Page_131]

Interestingly, a previous study showed an effect of fertilizer (i.e., ammonium sulphate fertilizer) on [SOURCE: Page_131]

Aedes and an impact on larval development, survival rates and larval density (Mutero et al. 2004; Muturi [SOURCE: Page_131]

et al. 2016; Darriet 2019). Moreover, Prud’homme et al. (2017) also revealed an impact of ibuprofen [SOURCE: Page_131]

and benzo[a]pyrene on life history traits and insecticide tolerance in Ae. aegypti (Prud’homme et al. [SOURCE: Page_131]

2017). More recently, Edwards et al. (2023) showed that microplastics decreased the bacterial and fungal [SOURCE: Page_131]

diversity of the Ae. albopictus and Ae. aegypti microbiota (Edwards et al. 2023). [SOURCE: Page_131]

We classified the community gardens according to their pollution context, which allowed us to [SOURCE: Page_131]

define pollution gradients that combined either the water physicochemical parameters and the pollution [SOURCE: Page_131]

variables (e.g., proximity to pollution sources) or water organic molecule occurrence. Our results [SOURCE: Page_131]

showed differential effects of pollution gradients according to the water colonization status. While no [SOURCE: Page_131]

relationship between physicochemical and pollution variables and bacterial composition was evidenced [SOURCE: Page_131]

in noncolonized water samples, the opposite was observed in colonized waters. Industrial pollution and [SOURCE: Page_131]

ionic composition were positively correlated with OTUs belonging to the bacterial genus [SOURCE: Page_131]

Actinomycetospora and fungal phylum Rozellomycota. Similarly, we found correlations between the [SOURCE: Page_131]

presence of organic molecules in water-colonized samples and OTUs belonging to the bacterial family [SOURCE: Page_131]

129 [SOURCE: Page_132]

Rhizobiaceae and the phytopathogenic fungus Ascochyta phaca. Actinomycetospora is common in [SOURCE: Page_132]

Aedes breeding sites (Zouache et al. 2022) and was found in this study to be highly prevalent in larvae. [SOURCE: Page_132]

The Rhizobiaceae family contains phenotypically diverse organisms, including N$_2$-fixing legume [SOURCE: Page_132]

symbionts (known as rhizobia), plant pathogens, bacterial predators, and other soil bacteria [SOURCE: Page_132]

(Kuzmanović et al. 2021). Interestingly, members of this family were already shown to be involved in [SOURCE: Page_132]

the degradation of bisphenol A and 4-nonylphenol, both ubiquitous pollutants with oestrogenic activity [SOURCE: Page_132]

in the aquatic environment (Cai et al. 2016; Gayathri and Da 2021). In noncolonized water samples, [SOURCE: Page_132]

Haematococcus was positively correlated with physicochemical and pollution variables. Interestingly, [SOURCE: Page_132]

Haematococcus are known to help to reduce water pollution, which may explain their presence in [SOURCE: Page_132]

polluted environments (Krishnamurthi et al. 2023). We also found specific patterns highlighting [SOURCE: Page_132]

correlations between the organic molecule gradient and OTUs identified as the green alga Neochloris [SOURCE: Page_132]

aquatica or as belonging to the fungal phylum Rozellomycota. Interestingly, Rozellomycota was also [SOURCE: Page_132]

correlated with pollution related to industrial sources and specific physicochemical profiles in colonized [SOURCE: Page_132]

waters. The Rozellomycota form a lineage basal or sister to fungi, the ancestor of Microsporidia [SOURCE: Page_132]

(Corsaro et al. 2014). Their biodiversity is very rich but remains poorly characterized. However, we [SOURCE: Page_132]

know that they are common in aquatic habitats and on pollen (Wurzbacher et al. 2014). They may play [SOURCE: Page_132]

a key role in the decomposition of organic matter (Grossart et al. 2016). The few known species are all [SOURCE: Page_132]

parasites, including water moulds and algae (Rozella), crustaceans (Mitosporidium), and endonuclear [SOURCE: Page_132]

parasites of amoebae (Nucleophaga, Paramicrosporidium) (Corsaro et al. 2020; Chung et al. 2020; [SOURCE: Page_132]

Gonçalves et al. 2022). Interestingly, Neochloris aquatica was shown to have a negative effect on the [SOURCE: Page_132]

larval development of Cx. quinquefasciatus mosquitoes (Gil et al. 2021). A study also showed their [SOURCE: Page_132]

ability to reduce or remove chemical oxygen demand (used as a measurement of pollutants in water), [SOURCE: Page_132]

nitrite, and phosphate from water (Ummalyma et al. 2023). Therefore, we could assume that this species [SOURCE: Page_132]

could modulate water physicochemical parameters that could in turn have consequences for colonization [SOURCE: Page_132]

by Ae. albopictus. Further studies under experimental conditions would help disentangle potential [SOURCE: Page_132]

interactions between the microbial taxa found to be correlated and the level of anthropization as well as [SOURCE: Page_132]

their impact on mosquito biology. [SOURCE: Page_132]

### 5. Conclusion

Our study confirmed that larval habitat is an important determinant of microbial community [SOURCE: Page_133]

structure. It also revealed that N2O and CH$_4$ were significantly higher in noncolonized water samples [SOURCE: Page_133]

than in colonized water samples. Then, we found that pollution gradients differentially contributed to [SOURCE: Page_133]

the microbial ecology of Ae. albopictus-colonized and Ae. albopictus-noncolonized water samples. The [SOURCE: Page_133]

relationships between microbial communities and water quality presented here are a first and simplified [SOURCE: Page_133]

step towards longitudinal sampling in the field and delimitation of the type of larval breeding sites in [SOURCE: Page_133]

the urban mosaic to determine their environmental exposome. Further studies are needed to determine [SOURCE: Page_133]

the influence of biotic and abiotic factors on the larval productivity of breeding sites in highly [SOURCE: Page_133]

anthropized environments and their carry-over impact on adult life history traits and vector competence. [SOURCE: Page_133]
